===== pillar-pseudo-code.txt =====

I'm using node, generating and generatign json content which I render to html in a later part of the pipeline, we can just focus on the creating of the json data files from our pillars

Perfect—since you’re in Node.js and the pipeline is:

Generate a JSON “draft” of the pillar page

Later, render that JSON → HTML

we can design a clean, schema‑aware JSON generator that produces the exact format you want for your pillars.

1. What the JSON generator should produce
For each pillar, your Node job should emit a single JSON object similar to the schema we agreed on, but focused just on the content plan, not CMS plumbing:

json
{
  "type": "pillar-page",
  "project": {...},
  "page": {...},
  "brief": {
    "promise": "...",
    "summary": "...",
    "sections": [
      {
        "section_id": "what-it-is",
        "h2": "What Book vs Movie Adaptation Means",
        "goal": "...",
        "key_points": [...],
        "h3s": [
          {"h3": "...", "goal": "..."}
        ]
      }
    ]
  },
  "clusters": [...],
  "qa": {...}
}
2. Algorithm: generate pillar JSON in Node (pseudocode)
Assume you have an array of pillar topics (e.g., the top‑10 list we discussed). Then:

js
function generatePillarJson(pillarTopic) {
  // Step 1: expand the pillar topic into a full brief object
  const brief = {
    project: {
      site_name: "Books Versus Movies",
      site_url: "https://booksversusmovies.com",
      primary_topic: "Book vs movie adaptations",
      secondary_topics: [
        "faithful adaptations",
        "movie changes from the book",
        "whether to read the book first"
      ],
    },

    page: {
      slug: pillarTopic.slug,          // e.g., "book-vs-movie-adaptations"
      h1: pillarTopic.h1,              // e.g., "Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide"
      title_tag: pillarTopic.titleTag,
      meta_description: pillarTopic.metaDescription,
      search_intent: "informational",
      audience: "Readers and viewers deciding how to engage with an adaptation",
      tone: "clear, opinionated, helpful",
    },

    brief: {
      // Step 2: use a mapping of topic → “starter” outline
      promise: getPillarPromise(pillarTopic.id),
      summary: getPillarSummary(pillarTopic.id),
      sections: getPillarSections(pillarTopic.id),
    },

    clusters: getRelatedClusters(pillarTopic.id), // e.g., an array of cluster briefs
    qa: {
      min_score: 85,
      checks: [
        "single_h1",
        "unique_intent",
        "no_cannibalization",
        "clear_internal_links",
        "seo_title_length",
        "meta_description_length",
        "reader_value",
        "outline_completeness"
      ]
    }
  };

  // Step 3: optionally validate against schema (not shown here)
  // validatePillarBrief(brief);  // using Ajv or similar

  return brief;
}
You can call this once per pillar in a loop:

js
const pillars = [
  { id: "adaptations-guide",    slug: "book-vs-movie-adaptations",       h1: "Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide", ... },
  { id: "best-adaptations",     slug: "best-book-to-movie-adaptations",  h1: "Best Book-to-Movie Adaptations: A Curated List",  ... },
  { id: "should-you-read-first", slug: "should-you-read-the-book-first", h1: "Should You Read the Book First?",               ... },
  // ... plus your 10 pillars
];

const pillarFiles = pillars.map(pillarTopic => {
  const pillarJson = generatePillarJson(pillarTopic);
  const filename = `pillar-${pillarTopic.slug}.json`;
  fs.writeFileSync(`data/${filename}`, JSON.stringify(pillarJson, null, 2));
  return { filename, slug: pillarTopic.slug };
});
3. How to build getPillarSections(topicId)
You can keep this logic in plain JS data (or a DB), for example:

js
const pillarSectionTemplates = {
  "adaptations-guide": [
    {
      section_id: "what-it-is",
      h2: "What Book vs Movie Adaptation Means",
      goal: "Define the topic and frame the site's perspective.",
      key_points: [
        "Explain why adaptations vary",
        "Set reader expectations",
        "Introduce the site's verdict-first approach"
      ],
      h3s: [
        { h3: "Why adaptations change between formats", goal: "Explain the practical reasons books become different movies." }
      ]
    },
    {
      section_id: "how-to-judge",
      h2: "How We Judge Adaptations",
      goal: "Define the evaluation criteria used across the site.",
      key_points: [
        "Faithfulness matters, but it is not the only factor",
        "Pacing and emotional impact matter too",
        "Different genres deserve different standards"
      ]
    }
    // ... more sections
  ],
  "best-adaptations": [
    {
      section_id: "best-overview",
      h2: "Best Book-to-Movie Adaptations: A Curated List",
      goal: "Show readers flagship examples of strong adaptations, with links to verdicts.",
      key_points: [
        "Quality, not quantity",
        "Mix of genres",
        "Clear reasons why each example stands out"
      ]
    }
    // ...
  ]
};
Then:

js
function getPillarSections(pillarId) {
  const template = pillarSectionTemplates[pillarId];
  if (!template) throw new Error(`No template for pillarId=${pillarId}`);
  return template;
}
You can still keep those “key points” short and let your LLM later turn them into full prose.

4. How to build getRelatedClusters(pillarId)
You can also keep a mapping of “which clusters belong under which pillar”:

js
const pillarClusterMap = {
  "adaptations-guide": [
    { slug: "best-book-to-movie-adaptations", h1: "Best Book-to-Movie Adaptations: A Curated List", intent: "informational", relationship_to_pillar: "Supports the pillar with a high-level list of great adaptation examples." },
    { slug: "should-you-read-the-book-first",  h1: "Should You Read the Book First?", intent: "informational", relationship_to_pillar: "Helps readers decide what to do first before exploring a title." }
    // ...
  ],
  "best-adaptations": [
    { slug: "best-book-to-movie-adaptations-scifi", h1: "Best Sci‑Fi Book‑to‑Movie Adaptations", ... },
    // ...
  ]
};

function getRelatedClusters(pillarId) {
  return pillarClusterMap[pillarId] || [];
}
Each cluster entry is a simple object; your later step (LLM or renderer) can expand it into a full page.

5. Putting it into a Node script
You can wrap this all up in a script that:

Has a list of pillars (const pillars = [...])

Runs generatePillarJson(pillar) for each

Writes pillar-<slug>.json into a data/pillars/ folder.

For example:

js
const fs = require("fs");

// pillar definitions + templates + mappings as above

function main() {
  const pillars = loadPillarDefinitions(); // your array
  for (const pillar of pillars) {
    const pillarJson = generatePillarJson(pillar);
    const outPath = `data/pillars/pillar-${pillar.slug}.json`;
    fs.mkdirSync("data/pillars", { recursive: true });
    fs.writeFileSync(outPath, JSON.stringify(pillarJson, null, 2));
    console.log(`✅ Written ${outPath}`);
  }
}

main();
If you want, I can next:

Turn one of your 10 pillars (e.g., “Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide”) into a complete Node‑style object literal you can drop straight into pillars = [...],
or

Write a real TypeScript / JS module with exported functions you can integrate into your pipeline.



===== pillar-schema.json =====

{
  "$schema": "https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/schema",
  "$id": "https://booksversusmovies.com/schemas/pillar-brief.schema.json",
  "title": "Pillar Page Brief",
  "type": "object",
  "additionalProperties": false,
  "required": ["project", "page", "brief", "clusters", "qa"],
  "properties": {
    "project": {
      "type": "object",
      "additionalProperties": false,
      "required": ["site_name", "site_url", "primary_topic"],
      "properties": {
        "site_name": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "site_url": { "type": "string", "format": "uri" },
        "primary_topic": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "secondary_topics": {
          "type": "array",
          "items": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 }
        }
      }
    },
    "page": {
      "type": "object",
      "additionalProperties": false,
      "required": ["slug", "h1", "title_tag", "meta_description", "search_intent", "audience"],
      "properties": {
        "slug": { "type": "string", "pattern": "^[a-z0-9]+(?:-[a-z0-9]+)*$" },
        "h1": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "title_tag": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1, "maxLength": 70 },
        "meta_description": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1, "maxLength": 160 },
        "search_intent": { "type": "string", "enum": ["informational", "commercial", "navigational", "transactional"] },
        "audience": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "tone": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 }
      }
    },
    "brief": {
      "type": "object",
      "additionalProperties": false,
      "required": ["promise", "summary", "sections"],
      "properties": {
        "promise": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "summary": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "sections": {
          "type": "array",
          "minItems": 3,
          "items": {
            "type": "object",
            "additionalProperties": false,
            "required": ["section_id", "h2", "goal", "key_points"],
            "properties": {
              "section_id": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
              "h2": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
              "goal": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
              "key_points": {
                "type": "array",
                "minItems": 1,
                "items": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 }
              },
              "h3s": {
                "type": "array",
                "items": {
                  "type": "object",
                  "additionalProperties": false,
                  "required": ["h3", "goal"],
                  "properties": {
                    "h3": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
                    "goal": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 }
                  }
                }
              }
            }
          }
        }
      }
    },
    "clusters": {
      "type": "array",
      "items": {
        "type": "object",
        "additionalProperties": false,
        "required": ["slug", "h1", "intent", "relationship_to_pillar"],
        "properties": {
          "slug": { "type": "string", "pattern": "^[a-z0-9]+(?:-[a-z0-9]+)*$" },
          "h1": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
          "intent": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
          "relationship_to_pillar": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
          "internal_links": {
            "type": "array",
            "items": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 }
          }
        }
      }
    },
    "qa": {
      "type": "object",
      "additionalProperties": false,
      "required": ["min_score", "checks"],
      "properties": {
        "min_score": { "type": "integer", "minimum": 0, "maximum": 100 },
        "checks": {
          "type": "array",
          "minItems": 1,
          "items": {
            "type": "string",
            "enum": [
              "single_h1",
              "unique_intent",
              "no_cannibalization",
              "clear_internal_links",
              "seo_title_length",
              "meta_description_length",
              "reader_value",
              "outline_completeness"
            ]
          }
        }
      }
    },
    "publish": {
      "type": "object",
      "additionalProperties": false,
      "properties": {
        "target": { "type": "string", "minLength": 1 },
        "status": { "type": "string", "enum": ["draft", "ready", "published"] }
      }
    }
  }
}

===== pillars-config-ideas.txt =====

You’re already thinking like a pipeline architect—using a pillars.json config to map “which model for which job” is a very clean, scalable pattern. Here’s how you can cleanly break it up, especially if you want Sonnet for outlines and Haiko (or another model) for detailed paragraphs.

1. High‑level split: “heavy” vs “light” tasks
Light model (e.g., Sonnet)

Prompt budget: structuring, outlining, decision‑making, short‑form QA.

Right‑fit jobs:

expanding a pillar topic into a JSON‑ready outline

adding H2/H3 skeletons

listing “key points” per section

Heavier model (e.g., Haiko / Opus / GPT‑4‑class)

Prompt budget: fluency, depth, voice consistency.

Right‑fit jobs:

turning an outline + key points into full prose paragraphs

maintaining tone and “opinionated verdict” style

lightly editing existing text and strengthening links CTAs

This keeps you from burning the big model on “what should the outline be?” and saves it for “make this actually read well.”

2. How to model your config
You can keep pillars.json as the master plan, but then add per‑task rules. For example:

json
{
  "pillars": [
    {
      "id": "adaptations-guide",
      "slug": "book-vs-movie-adaptations",
      "h1": "Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide",
      "title_tag": "Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide",
      "meta_description": "...",
      "steps": [
        {
          "role": "outline",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 800,
          "instructions": "Produce a JSON‑compatible outline with H2/H3 sections and key points."
        },
        {
          "role": "paragraphs",
          "model": "haiko",
          "max_tokens": 2000,
          "instructions": "Turn each section into opinionated, readable paragraphs, 300–500 words per section."
        },
        {
          "role": "linking",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 500,
          "instructions": "Add internal links to cluster pages and verdicts within the prose."
        }
      ]
    }
  ],
  "clusters": [
    {
      "id": "best-adaptations",
      "slug": "best-book-to-movie-adaptations",
      "steps": [
        {
          "role": "outline",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 600,
          "instructions": "Sketch a list‑style outline with categories and examples."
        },
        {
          "role": "paragraphs",
          "model": "haiko",
          "max_tokens": 1200,
          "instructions": "Write concise, punchy blurbs for each list item."
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
This way, your Node code can:

js
for (const pillar of config.pillars) {
  for (const step of pillar.steps) {
    const context = loadPillarContext(pillar.id); // previously generated JSON or outline
    const prompt = buildPrompt(step, context);
    const model = step.model;

    const rawOutput = await callModel(model, prompt, step.max_tokens);
    const normalizedOutput = normalizeOutput(step.role, rawOutput);

    saveIntermediate(step.role, pillar.id, normalizedOutput); // e.g., outline.json, paragraphs.json
  }
}
3. Concrete role / model mapping
outline → Sonnet‑class light model

Task:

Take a short topic + tone + target word count hints.

Output a structured JSON outline: H2/H3s + key points.

Why Sonnet is perfect:

Very good at “thinking step‑by‑step” without needing huge context windows.

paragraphs → Haiko / big model

Task:

Convert each section’s “key points” into smooth, opinionated paragraphs.

Maintain your site’s voice and drop natural internal links (e.g., “as explained in our faithful vs creative hub”).

Why the big model:

Consistency across long‑form text, tone, and linking strategy.

linking (optional) → Sonnet‑class

Task:

“Sprinkle” internal links into existing paragraphs.

Do not rewrite; just insert href‑style anchor text where it fits naturally.

Why light model: this is a “stitching” layer, not a deep rewrite.

You can also add:

qa or tone‑check step → big model

“Does this section match our tone and length target?”

“Suggest up to 3 edits that improve clarity.”

4. Folder structure: keep it pipeline‑friendly
You can mirror the split in your directories:

text
data/pillars/
  outline/               # .json outlines (Sonnet output)
    pillar-adaptations-guide.json
    pillar-best-adaptations.json

  paragraphs/            # .json with prose per section (Haiko output)
    pillar-adaptations-guide.json
    pillar-best-adaptations.json

  merged/                # final “ready‑to‑render” pillar.json (after stitching)
    pillar-adaptations-guide.json
Node pipeline stages:

Stage 1 (outline)

Read config/pillars.json

For each pillar, call Sonnet → write outline/pillar-<slug>.json.

Stage 2 (paragraphs)

Read the outline JSON.

Call Haiko per section → write paragraphs/pillar-<slug>.json.

Stage 3 (merge + linking)

Merge outline + paragraphs + cluster links.

Optionally call Sonnet again to “drop in links from clusters” → save merged/pillar-<slug>.json.

Stage 4 (render)

Your later job reads merged/pillar-<slug>.json and turns it into HTML.

5. Thoughts on language vs tools
If you’re already using JSON schemas, add one per role (e.g., outline.schema.json, paragraphs.schema.json) so each step is self‑contained and testable.

You can even have a “light” vs “heavy” gateway in code:

js
function pickModelForRole(role) {
  if (role === "outline" || role === "linking") return "sonnet";
  if (role === "paragraphs" || role === "tone‑check") return "haiko";
}
If you like, the next step can be:

I’ll draft a realistic config/pillars.json file for your top 3 pillars, with full steps arrays already filled out for Sonnet and Haiko roles,
or

I’ll give you a Node function skeleton that executes one of these roles based on a step object.



===== pillars.config.txt =====

{
  "site": {
    "name": "Books Versus Movies",
    "domain": "https://booksversusmovies.com",
    "primary_topic": "Book vs movie adaptations",
    "tone": "clear, opinionated, helpful"
  },

  "pillars": [
    {
      "id": "adaptations-guide",
      "slug": "book-vs-movie-adaptations",
      "h1": "Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide",
      "title_tag": "Book vs Movie Adaptations: The Ultimate Guide",
      "meta_description": "A practical guide to book vs movie adaptations, including how to judge faithfulness, changes, and which format to choose first.",
      "search_intent": "informational",
      "audience": "Readers and viewers deciding how to engage with an adaptation",

      "steps": [
        {
          "role": "outline",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 800,
          "instructions": "Produce a structured JSON‑compatible outline with H2 and H3 sections. Only include section id, H2/H3 text, and short key points. Do not write full paragraphs."
        },
        {
          "role": "paragraphs",
          "model": "haiko",
          "max_tokens": 2000,
          "instructions": "Turn each section in the outline into 1–2 fluent, opinionated paragraphs. Keep tone clear and helpful. Use 300–500 words per section. Add 1–2 natural internal links to cluster pages per section."
        },
        {
          "role": "linking",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 500,
          "instructions": "Review the paragraphs. Add or fix 1–2 extra internal links per section pointing to cluster pages and verdict pages. Do not rewrite sentences; only insert or adjust links."
        }
      ]
    },

    {
      "id": "best-adaptations",
      "slug": "best-book-to-movie-adaptations",
      "h1": "Best Book‑to‑Movie Adaptations: A Curated List",
      "title_tag": "Best Book‑to‑Movie Adaptations: Curated by Verdict",
      "meta_description": "A hand‑picked list of the best book‑to‑movie adaptations, with brief explanations and links to our full book vs movie verdicts.",
      "search_intent": "informational",
      "audience": "Movie lovers and book fans deciding which adaptations to try first",

      "steps": [
        {
          "role": "outline",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 700,
          "instructions": "Create a list‑style outline with sections such as 'Why Some Adaptations Stand Out' and 'Genre‑by‑Genre Picks'. For each list item, add a short key point explaining why it's strong. Do not write full paragraphs."
        },
        {
          "role": "paragraphs",
          "model": "haiko",
          "max_tokens": 1500,
          "instructions": "Turn each list item and section into concise, punchy blurbs (50–100 words each). Keep the tone light and verdict‑driven. Link each title to its verdict page."
        },
        {
          "role": "linking",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 400,
          "instructions": "Ensure each example in the list links to its verdict page. Add 1–2 links to related cluster pages (e.g., faithful vs creative, changes from book to movie)."
        }
      ]
    },

    {
      "id": "should-you-read-first",
      "slug": "should-you-read-the-book-first",
      "h1": "Should You Read the Book First? The Book vs Movie Guide",
      "title_tag": "Should You Read the Book First? A Decision Guide",
      "meta_description": "A decision‑oriented guide explaining when to read the book first, when to watch the movie first, and when to do both.",
      "search_intent": "informational",
      "audience": "Readers and viewers who are unsure how to start an adaptation",

      "steps": [
        {
          "role": "outline",
          "model": "sonnet",
          "max_tokens": 700,
          "instructions": "Build an outline with H2s like 'Reasons to Read First', 'Reasons to Watch First', and 'A Quick Decision Checklist'. Add 3–5 short key points per section. Do not write full paragraphs."
        },
        {
          "role": "paragraphs",
          "model": "haiko",
          "max_tokens": 1800,

===== reference\POLICIES.md =====

# BooksVersusMovies.com — Pipeline Policies
==========================================
Last updated: April 2026

This document covers hard rules, conventions, and guardrails for the pipeline.
These are decisions that have been made and should not be changed without
understanding the consequences. For architectural context see README.md.


## LLM Policies

### Never hardcode model names in scripts
All model assignments live in config/models.json. Scripts read this file at
startup and log which model is being used. Changing a model requires only
editing config/models.json — no code changes.

### LLM never touches HTML
The LLM reads and writes JSON only. The renderer (pipeline-render.js) converts
JSON to HTML deterministically. This is non-negotiable — it prevents broken
markup, enables model swapping, and guarantees structural consistency.

### Immutable fields
The following fields must never be changed by any LLM pass. They are set
during extraction and are the source of truth:
  slug, filename, pipelineVersion, bookTitle, author, bookYear, filmYear,
  affiliateLink, youtubeId, verdictText, verdictClass, storyBrief, verdictBox,
  relatedSectionTitle

### Temperature guidelines
- 0.3 — structural passes where consistency matters most (Pass 1)
- 0.4 — conversion passes with some creative latitude (Pass 2, titles)
- 0.5 — generation passes where voice matters (greenfield)
- Never above 0.7 — this site requires consistency, not surprise


## Pipeline Policies

### Pass execution order
Passes must always run in this order: Pass 1 → Titles → Pass 2
Each pass depends on the output of the previous. Running out of order
produces incomplete or incorrect JSON.

### Input directory rules
- Pass 1 always reads from pipeline/1-extracted/ (raw source)
- Titles and Pass 2 always read from pipeline/2-revised/ (or in-memory
  current when running --pass all in a single invocation)
- Never run titles or Pass 2 against pipeline/1-extracted/ directly

### Never modify pipeline/1-extracted/
This directory is the permanent raw extraction source. It is never modified
by any pipeline script. If extraction needs to be re-run, use --force on
pipeline-extract.js which will overwrite 1-extracted/ from the source HTML.

### Checkpoint gate
Do not proceed to the next pipeline step if the checkpoint fails.
A failed checkpoint indicates a content, validation, or structural issue
that will propagate through the renderer and into the live site.

### Batch commit before push
Commit locally as often as you like. Push only when a section or deployment
batch is complete. This minimises Netlify build charges and keeps deploy
history clean.


## API Policies

### Socket timeout
All HTTP requests to OpenRouter must include a socket timeout (currently 90s).
Without this, a dropped connection mid-response causes the process to hang
indefinitely — the promise never rejects so the retry logic never fires.

### Retry policy
- 3 attempts maximum
- Exponential backoff: 10s, 20s
- Do NOT retry: 401, 400, user not found, invalid API key
- DO retry: ECONNRESET, ETIMEDOUT, 429, 500, 503

### API key security
- OPENROUTER_API_KEY lives in .env only — never committed to git
- .env is in .gitignore
- If a key is accidentally committed, rotate it immediately — scrapers
  monitor GitHub in real time and will find it within minutes
- Verify .env is not tracked: git check-ignore -v .env


## Deployment Policies

### Pre-deployment checklist
1. Run extract-metadata.js — confirm 0 problematic files
2. Confirm CHECKPOINT PASSED on renderer run
3. Spot-check 5-10 rendered pages in browser
4. Confirm affiliate links and YouTube IDs are intact
5. Copy pipeline/3-rendered/*.html to project root
6. Copy pipeline/2-revised/*.json to data/reviews/
7. Push to git — unlock Netlify auto-publish or manually publish deploy

### URL structure
Live pages are served at: https://booksversusmovies.com/slug
No .html extension (Netlify Pretty URLs). No /reviews/ subdirectory.
The renderer must output canonical tags in this format:
  <link rel="canonical" href="https://booksversusmovies.com/slug">

### CSS and image paths
Rendered HTML must use root-relative paths:
  css/style.css       ✓
  images/slug.jpg     ✓
  ../css/style.css    ✗  (wrong — pages deploy to root, not reviews/)
  ../images/          ✗  (wrong)


## Content Policies

### Quarantine policy
Pages with structural problems go to reviews-to-review/ and are excluded
from all pipeline runs. Document the reason in docs/pipeline-notes.md.
Do not attempt to process quarantined pages until the issue is resolved.

### Affiliate links
Every page must have a valid affiliateLink field. The renderer uses this
for the book cover link, buy button, and all three CTA blocks. A missing
or invalid affiliate link means the page generates no revenue.

### CTA text rules
after-quick-answer:  "Read the book first →"           (Book Wins)
                     "Get the book →"                   (Too Close to Call)
                     "Read the source material →"       (Movie Wins)
after-read-first:    "Get the book on Amazon →"         (all verdicts)
after-verdict:       "Start with [Title] →"             (bookTitle ≤ 20 chars)
                     "Start with the book →"            (bookTitle > 20 chars)


## Claude Output Policy
When delivering files in Claude responses, always state the exact
destination path for each file before or after presenting it.

Format:
  filename.js  →  scripts/
  prompt.txt   →  scripts/prompts/
  data.json    →  data/

This applies to all new files and updated files. Never assume the
user knows where a file goes based on context alone.


===== reference\README.md =====

# BooksVersusMovies.com — Developer README
==========================================
Last updated: April 2026

This document covers project conventions, best practices, and architectural
decisions for the BooksVersusMovies pipeline. Read this before making changes
to any pipeline script or configuration.


## Project Structure

```
movies/
├── config/
│   └── models.json          ← LLM model assignments (edit here, not in scripts)
├── css/
│   └── style.css
├── data/
│   ├── extracted_metadata.csv
│   ├── metadata-issues.csv
│   ├── reports/
│   │   ├── gsc/             ← Google Search Console exports
│   │   └── analytics/       ← Google Analytics exports
│   └── reviews/             ← canonical JSON source of truth (post-pipeline)
├── docs/
│   ├── action-plan.txt
│   ├── business-strategy.md
│   ├── completed-tasks.md   ← append here after every completed task
│   ├── new-ideas.md         ← add ideas here for next-steps script to surface
│   ├── pipeline-notes.md
│   ├── project-objectives.md
│   └── review-schema.md     ← canonical JSON schema definition
├── pipeline/
│   ├── 1-extracted/         ← html-to-json output (raw)
│   ├── 2-revised/           ← pipeline-revise output (LLM revised)
│   └── 3-rendered/          ← pipeline-render output (final HTML)
├── reviews/                 ← live deployed HTML pages
├── reviews-to-review/       ← quarantine folder for problem pages
├── scripts/
│   ├── prompts/
│   │   ├── pass1-structural.txt
│   │   ├── pass2-conversion.txt
│   │   └── greenfield.txt
│   ├── extract-metadata.js  ← utility: site audit and QA
│   ├── generate-next-steps.js ← utility: project briefing
│   ├── logger.js            ← shared module (do not run directly)
│   ├── pipeline-extract.js  ← pipeline: HTML → JSON
│   ├── pipeline-generate.js ← pipeline: greenfield new reviews
│   ├── pipeline-render.js   ← pipeline: JSON → HTML
│   ├── pipeline-revise.js   ← pipeline: LLM revision passes
│   └── tree.js              ← utility: project structure viewer
└── logs/                    ← one log file per script run (auto-generated)
```


## Naming Conventions

### Scripts
Two categories — prefix signals which type:

`pipeline-*.js` — production workflow scripts run regularly:
- `pipeline-extract.js` — converts HTML to JSON (bootstrap or re-extraction)
- `pipeline-revise.js`  — runs LLM revision passes (Pass 1 + Pass 2)
- `pipeline-render.js`  — renders JSON to HTML for deployment
- `pipeline-generate.js`— generates new reviews from scratch (greenfield)

Utility scripts (no prefix) — run occasionally for maintenance:
- `extract-metadata.js`    — full site audit, produces metadata CSV
- `generate-next-steps.js` — project briefing, reads all docs and data
- `tree.js`                — prints project structure

### Files
- Log files: `YYYY-MM-DD_HH-MM-SS_<scriptname>.log`
- GSC/Analytics reports: `YYYY-MM-description.csv`
- Review JSON: `<slug>.json` (e.g. `atonement.json`)

### Git commits
Keep commit messages short and specific. Format:
`<scope>: <what changed>`

Examples:
- `pipeline-revise: add OpenRouter support`
- `config: add models.json`
- `step 2 complete: schema, logger, extractor`


## Configuration

### LLM Models — config/models.json
**Never hardcode model names in scripts.** All model assignments live in
`config/models.json`. Scripts read this file at startup.

```json
{
  "pass1": {
    "model": "anthropic/claude-haiku-4-5",
    "maxTokens": 4096,
    "temperature": 0.3
  },
  "pass2": {
    "model": "anthropic/claude-haiku-4-5",
    "maxTokens": 2048,
    "temperature": 0.4
  },
  "greenfield": {
    "model": "anthropic/claude-sonnet-4-5",
    "maxTokens": 6000,
    "temperature": 0.5
  }
}
```

To test a different model: edit `models.json`, run, compare output, revert
if worse. No code changes required.

Temperature guidance:
- 0.3 — structural passes where consistency matters most
- 0.4-0.5 — conversion and generation passes with some creative latitude
- Never above 0.7 — this site requires consistency, not surprise

### API Keys — .env
All secrets live in `.env` in the project root. Never commit this file.
`.env` is in `.gitignore`.

```
OPENROUTER_API_KEY=your-key-here
```

Scripts load `.env` automatically using a lightweight built-in parser.
No `dotenv` package required.

To override a model for a single run without editing `models.json`:
```cmd
set PASS1_MODEL=anthropic/claude-sonnet-4-5 && node scripts\pipeline-revise.js --slug atonement
```


## Pipeline Architecture

### The content/structure divorce
**The LLM never touches HTML.** This is non-negotiable.

- LLM reads and writes JSON only
- The renderer (pipeline-render.js) converts JSON to HTML deterministically
- HTML in `reviews/` is always renderer output — never hand-edit it

This means:
- Any model can safely process the JSON without risk of broken markup
- You can swap models and compare output without touching HTML
- Structure is guaranteed consistent across all 163+ pages

### Pipeline flow
```
reviews/*.html
    ↓ pipeline-extract.js
pipeline/1-extracted/*.json     ← raw extraction, never modified
    ↓ pipeline-revise.js
pipeline/2-revised/*.json       ← LLM revised (Pass 1 → Pass 2)
    ↓ pipeline-render.js
pipeline/3-rendered/*.html      ← final HTML, ready for deployment
    ↓ (manual approval)
reviews/*.html                  ← live pages
data/reviews/*.json             ← canonical JSON, copied from 2-revised/
```

### What each pass does
Pass 1 (structural overlay):
- Adds `quickAnswer` block
- Optionally improves `pageTitle` for CTR
- Optionally tightens FAQ questions
- Does NOT touch: storyBrief, differences, readFirst, verdictBox

Pass 2 (conversion layer):
- Adds `ctaBlocks` — mid-article CTA placements
- Does NOT touch any prose fields

### Source of truth
`data/reviews/*.json` is the canonical source once the pipeline is complete.
`pipeline/` folders are working scratch space — safe to delete and regenerate.
`reviews/*.html` is renderer output — safe to regenerate from `data/reviews/`.


## Prompts

Prompts live in `scripts/prompts/` as plain `.txt` files. This separation means:
- Edit prompts without touching code
- Version prompts independently in git
- Test prompt variants by duplicating the file and pointing to it

### Prompt versioning
When making significant prompt changes:
1. Copy the existing prompt: `pass1-structural.txt` → `pass1-structural-v2.txt`
2. Edit the new version
3. Test on 3-5 pages before committing
4. If better, rename to replace the original
5. Log the change in `docs/pipeline-notes.md` under "Prompt Change Log"

### Prompt writing rules
- Lead with voice and tone before structure requirements
- Use concrete good/bad examples for subjective judgements
- List immutable fields explicitly — models occasionally drift
- Require JSON-only output with no preamble or markdown fences
- Keep system prompts under 2,000 tokens where possible


## Logging

Every pipeline script produces a timestamped log file in `logs/`.
Logs capture: every file processed, warnings, errors, checkpoint results,
elapsed time.

Log files are never overwritten — each run produces a new file.
Use logs to:
- Debug unexpected output from LLM passes
- Compare runs across different models
- Audit what ran and when before deployment

`logs/` is committed to git so you have a permanent record.
Consider adding a periodic cleanup task once logs accumulate.


## Checkpoints

Every pipeline script ends with a checkpoint block that prints
`CHECKPOINT PASSED` or `CHECKPOINT FAILED`.

**Do not proceed to the next pipeline step if the checkpoint fails.**

Checkpoint failures indicate either:
- A content extraction problem (fix in pipeline-extract.js)
- An LLM validation failure (inspect the specific file, adjust prompt)
- A structural issue in the source HTML (move to reviews-to-review/)

Add `--strict` flag to any script to exit with code 1 on checkpoint failure,
which is useful if you ever automate the pipeline.


## Reviews to Review

`reviews-to-review/` is the quarantine folder for pages that need manual
attention before they can go through the pipeline.

Current contents and reasons are tracked in `docs/pipeline-notes.md`
under "Known Issues and Decisions".

Do not run pipeline scripts against files in `reviews-to-review/` — they
are excluded from all pipeline runs by default.


## Adding a New Review

New reviews enter the pipeline via `pipeline-generate.js` (greenfield),
not via hand-written HTML. This ensures every new page is born with the
full structure the pipeline expects.

Until `pipeline-generate.js` is built (Step 5), new reviews can be
hand-written and then run through `pipeline-extract.js` to produce JSON,
followed by the revision passes.


## Deployment

Before deploying:
1. Run `extract-metadata.js` as a final QA pass
2. Confirm `CHECKPOINT PASSED` with 0 problematic files
3. Confirm affiliate links and YouTube IDs are intact
4. Spot-check 5-10 rendered pages in a browser
5. Copy `pipeline/3-rendered/*.html` to `reviews/`
6. Copy `pipeline/2-revised/*.json` to `data/reviews/`
7. Push to git — Netlify deploys automatically

**Batch commits before pushing to minimise Netlify build charges.**
Commit locally as often as you like. Push only when a section or
deployment batch is complete.


## API Resilience Best Practices

### Socket timeout
All HTTP requests to OpenRouter include a 90-second socket timeout. Without
this, a dropped connection mid-response causes the process to hang indefinitely
— the retry logic never fires because the promise never rejects.

If you see a pipeline run that appears frozen with no output for several
minutes, the socket timeout wasn't firing. Check that the timeout is set in
callOpenRouterOnce() in pipeline-revise.js.

### Retry logic
Three attempts with exponential backoff: 10s, 20s. Total retry window is
~30 seconds per attempt set. Auth errors (401, user not found) are not
retried since they require manual intervention.

Do NOT retry on:
- OPENROUTER_API_KEY expired or invalid
- 400 bad request (prompt or payload issue)
- 401 unauthorized

DO retry on:
- ECONNRESET (connection dropped)
- ETIMEDOUT (request timed out)
- 429 rate limit (back off and retry)
- 500/503 server errors

### Environment-based model config (planned)
Currently all model assignments live in config/models.json. The plan is to
support config/models.test.json and config/models.dev.json with a --env flag:

  node scripts\pipeline-revise.js --all --pass all --env test   ← DeepSeek
  node scripts\pipeline-revise.js --all --pass all --env prod   ← Haiku/Sonnet

Script falls back to config/models.json if env-specific file doesn't exist.
See docs/new-ideas.md for implementation notes.

### Resume capability
All pipeline scripts support resume after interruption. Without --force,
scripts skip files that already exist in the output directory. For pipeline-
revise.js, the smart skip logic checks whether required fields are populated
rather than just whether the file exists — so a file missing ctaBlocks will
be re-processed even without --force.


## Key Decisions Log

| Date       | Decision                                              | Reason                                      |
|------------|-------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| 2026-04-11 | LLM never touches HTML                                | Prevents broken markup, enables model swap  |
| 2026-04-11 | Model config in config/models.json                    | Change models without touching code         |
| 2026-04-11 | OpenRouter as API provider                            | Model flexibility, cost comparison          |
| 2026-04-11 | pipeline-* prefix for production scripts              | Clear distinction from utility scripts      |
| 2026-04-11 | director field optional for series pages              | Series have no single director              |
| 2026-04-11 | bookCoverImage stores filename only                   | Renderer constructs path — portable JSON    |
| 2026-04-11 | quickAnswer/ctaBlocks null until pipeline sets them   | Extracted JSON reflects current page state  |
| 2026-04-11 | Batch git commits before push                         | Minimise Netlify build charges              |
| 2026-04-11 | data/reviews/ is canonical source post-pipeline       | pipeline/ folders are scratch space         |

BooksVersusMovies.com — Pipeline Action Plan
=============================================

1. Housekeeping
   1.1 Move wild.html to reviews-to-review
   1.2 Update extract-metadata.js to exclude non-review files, treat director as optional
       for series pages, and handle non-standard book years like "800 BC"
   1.3 Re-run extractor to produce a clean baseline metadata.csv in data/

2. Define the content/structure divorce
   2.1 Design the JSON schema that all pipeline operations will read and write —
       one file per review, stored in data/reviews/
   2.2 Write a one-time extraction script that converts existing HTML pages into
       their JSON representations
   2.3 Add the quick-answer CSS component to style.css

3. Build the revision pipeline
   3.1 Write Pass 1 prompt — structural overlay: generates quick-answer block,
       verifies FAQ quality, checks CTA placement
   3.2 Write the Node pipeline script that reads a JSON file, runs Pass 1 via the
       Anthropic API, and writes the result back to JSON
   3.3 Single-page prompt evaluation loop: run Pass 1 on one page, read JSON output,
       judge quality, adjust prompt, repeat before scaling
   3.4 Write Pass 2 prompt — conversion layer: improves CTA wording, adds mid-article
       CTAs after decision moments
   3.5 Extend the pipeline script to run Pass 2 after Pass 1
   3.6 Test on five pages, review output quality manually before running at scale

4. Build the renderer
   4.1 Write a minimal renderer that handles the existing page structure without
       the quick-answer block — enables visual validation during prompt development
   4.2 Extend the renderer to handle the full revised structure including
       quick-answer block and updated CTA placements
   4.3 Validate rendered output against three known-good pages to confirm fidelity
   4.4 Run renderer across all files in data/reviews/ and write output to reviews/

5. Build the greenfield pipeline
   5.1 Design the input data format — the structured fields a new review needs
       before any LLM is involved
   5.2 Write the generation prompt that produces a complete review JSON from
       input data
   5.3 Wire the greenfield prompt into the same renderer from step 4 so new and
       revised pages share one render path

6. Validate and ship
   6.1 Spot-check 10 revised pages against originals for voice, accuracy,
       and structure
   6.2 Confirm affiliate links, YouTube IDs, and related cards are intact
       across all pages
   6.3 Re-run extractor as a final QA pass to confirm no missing fields
       across the full set
   6.4 Deploy and monitor GSC for CTR changes over 4 weeks

give me a git cheat sheet. I need to switch to dev and get lastest from prod

3:38 PM
cmd
git checkout dev
git merge main
That's it for this case. Here's the broader cheat sheet for your workflow:

Daily workflow:

cmd
git checkout dev          (switch to dev)
git merge main            (get latest from prod)
Save work on dev:

cmd
git add .
git commit -m "description"
Deploy to prod:

cmd
git checkout main
git merge dev
git push
git checkout dev          (switch back to dev immediately)
Check where you are:

cmd
git branch                (shows current branch with *)
git status                (shows uncommitted changes)
git log --oneline -5      (last 5 commits)
Undo last commit (keep changes):

cmd
git reset --soft HEAD~1
Discard all uncommitted changes:

cmd
git checkout -- .
See what changed:

cmd
git diff                  (unstaged changes)
git diff --staged         (staged changes)
The main rule for your setup: always do work on dev, only touch main to merge and push, then immediately switch back to dev.

===== reference\greenfield-happy-path.txt =====

GREENFIELD HAPPY PATH — BooksVersusMovies.com
=============================================
How to generate a new review from scratch.
Replace "the-godfather" and "The Godfather" with your slug and title.


STEP 1 — Create input file
--------------------------
Create: data/greenfield/inputs/the-godfather.json

{
  "bookTitle": "The Godfather",
  "slug": "the-godfather",
  "affiliateLink": "https://amzn.to/xxxxx",
  "youtubeId": "sY1S34973zA",
  "videoAffiliateLink": "https://amzn.to/xxxxx"
}

Slug rules: lowercase, hyphens only, no spaces. e.g. "the-godfather"
youtubeId:  just the ID, not the full URL. e.g. "sY1S34973zA"


STEP 2 — Stage 1: factual extraction (Haiku)
--------------------------------------------
node scripts\pipeline-generate.js --slug the-godfather --stage 1

REVIEW output: data/greenfield/stage1/the-godfather.json
Check: author, bookYear, filmYear, director are all correct.
If wrong: edit the stage1 file manually before running stage 2.


STEP 3 — Stage 2: full review generation (Sonnet/Haiku)
--------------------------------------------------------
node scripts\pipeline-generate.js --slug the-godfather --stage 2

REVIEW output: data/greenfield/stage2/the-godfather.json
Check: verdict, oneLineReason, differences, characters all look right.
File is also copied to: pipeline/2-revised/the-godfather.json


STEP 4 — Titles pass (Sonnet)
------------------------------
node scripts\pipeline-revise.js --slug the-godfather --pass titles --force

Check: pageTitle is sharp and under 65 chars. metaDesc is 130-155 chars.


STEP 5 — Pass 2: CTA blocks (Haiku)
-------------------------------------
node scripts\pipeline-revise.js --slug the-godfather --pass 2 --force

NOTE: use "--pass 2" not "--pass pass2"


STEP 6 — Validate
------------------
node scripts\validate-json-schema.js --slug the-godfather --schema revised

Must pass before rendering. Fix any errors and re-run the relevant pass.


STEP 7 — Add book cover image
------------------------------
Copy the-godfather.jpg to images/
Recommended size: 400x600px minimum, JPG format.
Filename must match the slug exactly: the-godfather.jpg


STEP 8 — Render
----------------
node scripts\pipeline-render.js --slug the-godfather --force

Check: checkpoint must pass all 6 checks including CTA blocks.


STEP 9 — Copy to root
----------------------
xcopy pipeline\3-rendered\the-godfather.html . /Y


STEP 10 — Commit and deploy
----------------------------
git add .
git commit -m "Add The Godfather review"
git checkout main
git merge dev
git push
git checkout dev


TROUBLESHOOTING
---------------
Pass 2 not running?        Use --pass 2 not --pass pass2
CTA blocks still null?     Use --pass 2 not --pass pass2
Render fails with replace? A field has wrong type — run validate first
JSON parse error?          jsonrepair will handle most cases, re-run stage 2
inBook/inFilm boolean?     Stage 2 prompt issue — re-run stage 2 with --force
Stage 2 skipped?           Add --force flag


===== reference\pipeline-notes.md =====

# BooksVersusMovies.com — Pipeline Notes

## OVERVIEW

The pipeline converts review content through three stages before deployment.
Each stage has a dedicated folder under pipeline/ for inspection and debugging.

pipeline/1-extracted/   Raw JSON extracted from existing HTML pages
pipeline/2-revised/     JSON after LLM revision passes (Pass 1 + Pass 2)
pipeline/3-rendered/    Final HTML ready for deployment to reviews/

The canonical source of truth for all review content is data/reviews/.
Once the pipeline is running, HTML files in reviews/ should never be
hand-edited — all changes go through the pipeline.



## PIPELINE STAGES

Stage 1 — Extraction (html-to-json.js)
Input:  reviews/*.html
Output: pipeline/1-extracted/*.json
Notes:  One-time operation to bootstrap the JSON layer from existing HTML.
Subsequent new reviews enter at data/reviews/ directly via the
greenfield pipeline (generate.js).

Stage 2 — Revision (revise.js)
Input:  pipeline/1-extracted/*.json  (or data/reviews/*.json for re-runs)
Output: pipeline/2-revised/\*.json
Passes:
Pass 1 — Structural overlay

* Generates quick-answer block
* Verifies FAQ quality and coverage
* Checks CTA placement against decision moments
Pass 2 — Conversion layer
* Improves CTA wording (intent-driven language)
* Adds mid-article CTAs after answer, recommendation, and verdict sections
Notes:  Run Pass 1 on a single page and review JSON output before scaling.
Adjust prompt before running Pass 2 or processing more pages.

Stage 3 — Render (render.js)
Input:  pipeline/2-revised/*.json
Output: pipeline/3-rendered/*.html
Notes:  Deterministic — no LLM involvement. Structure, CSS classes, affiliate
link injection, and schema markup are all controlled here.
Validate against three known-good pages before full run.



## DEPLOYMENT

When pipeline/3-rendered/ output is approved:

* Copy files to reviews/
* Re-run extract-metadata.js as final QA pass
* Confirm no missing fields in metadata.csv before publishing



## PROMPT VERSIONING

Prompts are stored as plain text in scripts/prompts/ so they can be edited
independently of the pipeline code.

pass1-structural.txt    — Pass 1 system + user prompt
pass2-conversion.txt    — Pass 2 system + user prompt
greenfield.txt          — New review generation prompt

When modifying prompts, note the version, date, and reason for change below.

## Prompt Change Log

\[date] \[file] \[version] — \[reason for change]



## MODEL ROUTING

Current assignments (update as testing informs decisions):

Extraction (html-to-json.js)  — not LLM-dependent
Pass 1 structural              — TBD
Pass 2 conversion              — TBD
Greenfield generation          — TBD

Notes on model evaluation:

* Test Pass 1 and Pass 2 with at least two models before committing
* Evaluate on: voice preservation, structural consistency, CTA quality
* Cheaper models are acceptable for Pass 2 (CTA/FAQ) if voice holds



## KNOWN ISSUES AND DECISIONS

* director field is optional for TV series pages (series have no single director)
* book year "800 BC" and similar non-standard dates require special handling
in extract-metadata.js
* wild.html moved to reviews-to-review — related cards contained placeholder slugs
* spotlight.html deleted — was index page spotlight feature, not a review
* twilight.html and unbroken.html moved to reviews-to-review — v1 generation,
missing FAQ and character table, will be regenerated via greenfield pipeline
* dune.html / dune-improved.html and similar duplicate slugs deferred to
reviews-to-review for manual resolution



We want to make sure netify continues to serve pages at https://booksversusmovies.com/{moviename} without the /reviews

pipeline/3-rendered/\*.html  →  copy to project root

pipeline/2-revised/\*.json   →  copy to data/reviews/



For the action plan going forward — add a note to docs/pipeline-notes.md that the pipeline should eventually be refactored to use per-pass subdirectories. For now, the --pass all approach is the pragmatic fix.



===== reference\project-objectives.md =====

BooksVersusMovies.com — Project Objectives
==========================================

WHAT THIS SITE IS
-----------------
BooksVersusMovies.com publishes comparison pages for books and their film or
television adaptations. Each page answers three questions a reader actually
has: which version is better, what are the meaningful differences, and should
I read the book before watching.

The site exists at the intersection of two things: a genuine editorial interest
in how stories change when they move between media, and a scalable affiliate
revenue model built on Amazon book purchases. The writing should be good enough
that the site is worth reading. The structure should be optimised enough that
it ranks, gets clicked, and converts.

The goal is not to be a listicle factory. It is to be the smartest, most
opinionated comparison resource on the internet for this specific niche —
the kind of site you would bookmark and return to, written with the
specificity and confidence of someone who has actually read the books and
watched the films.


REVENUE MODEL
-------------
Primary: Amazon Associates affiliate commissions on book purchases
  - Affiliate links on every page via buy button and book cover image
  - CTAs placed at decision moments: after quick answer, after recommendation,
    after verdict
  - Target: reader who has just decided the book is worth reading clicks
    through and buys

Secondary (future consideration): film/streaming affiliate links where available


CONTENT PHILOSOPHY
------------------
Voice
  Precise, opinionated, no hedging. Write like a knowledgeable critic, not a
  book report. Strong verdicts. Specific references to characters, scenes,
  actors, and techniques. No "both versions have their merits" without
  immediately saying which merits matter more.

Structure
  Structured for search, written for humans. The template handles SEO mechanics
  — quick answer, comparison table, FAQ — so the writing doesn't have to carry
  that weight. The writing lives in the difference sections and the verdict.

Tone
  The Atlantic-style criticism inside an SEO-optimised shell. Not BuzzFeed.
  Not an academic essay. A smart friend with taste who has read the book and
  watched the film and will tell you which to do first.

What to avoid
  - Hedging verdicts to avoid taking a position
  - Generic observations that apply to any adaptation ("the book has more detail")
  - Padding differences with plot summary
  - Listicle language and manufactured urgency


SEO AND CONVERSION TARGETS
---------------------------
Primary search intents this site targets:
  - "[title] book vs movie"
  - "should I read [title] before watching"
  - "differences between [title] book and movie"
  - "is [title] movie faithful to the book"
  - "[title] which is better book or movie"

Funnel targets (current baseline, from GSC):
  - CTR: currently ~1.3%, target 3-6% after revision pipeline
  - Impressions to first sale: currently ~5,000+, target ~1,200 after optimisation

Ranking priority:
  - Pages currently sitting at position 5-12 are the highest ROI targets
  - Push these to top 5 before worrying about new content


CURRENT SCALE
-------------
  ~170 live review pages
  Coverage: literary fiction, thriller, YA, romance, sci-fi, memoir,
            historical fiction, horror, fantasy


PIPELINE OBJECTIVE
------------------
The pipeline exists to make the site's performance predictable and scalable.

Revision pipeline
  Takes existing pages and brings them up to the full structural spec:
  quick-answer block, optimised CTAs, verified FAQ coverage. Goal is to
  unlock value from pages already ranking but not converting.

Greenfield pipeline
  Produces new pages that are born fully optimised — no revision pass needed.
  Goal is to scale coverage without scaling manual effort.

Both pipelines share one renderer, ensuring every page on the site has
identical structure regardless of how it was produced.


SUCCESS METRICS
---------------
Short term (4 weeks post-deployment)
  - CTR improvement on revised pages vs pre-revision baseline
  - First affiliate sale

Medium term (3 months)
  - Pages per month ranking in top 5 for primary keyword
  - Revenue per 1,000 impressions

Long term
  - Direct traffic and return visitors (indicator of brand, not just SEO)
  - Expanding coverage to 300+ titles via greenfield pipeline

# BooksVersusMovies.com — Project Structure
============================================
Last updated: April 14, 2026

This is the authoritative reference for the current project layout after the transition to a full JSON-first pipeline with greenfield generation.

### Core Architecture Principles
- **JSON is the source of truth** — All review content lives in structured JSON files.
- **LLM never touches HTML** — Only the renderer (`pipeline-render.js`) generates HTML.
- **Separation of concerns** — Prompts, config, data stages, and rendering are isolated.
- **Pipeline is linear and idempotent** — Extract → Generate/Revise → Render → Deploy.

### Directory Overview

#### Root (`/`)
Contains all live, deployed files served by Netlify with pretty URLs.
- `index.html` — Main landing page (generated by `pipeline-browse.js`)
- Individual review pages (`the-godfather.html`, `verity.html`, etc.)
- `auteurs.html`, `about.html`, `welcome.html`
- `sitemap.xml`, `robots.txt`, `package.json`

#### `scripts/`
All executable Node.js code.
- **Core Pipeline Scripts**:
  - `pipeline-generate.js` — Greenfield two-stage generator (Stage 1 + Stage 2)
  - `pipeline-revise.js` — Pass 1, Titles pass, Pass 2
  - `pipeline-render.js` — JSON → final HTML (deterministic)
  - `pipeline-browse.js` — Generates `index.html`
  - `pipeline-extract.js` — Legacy HTML → JSON bootstrap
- **Supporting Scripts**:
  - `validate-json-schema.js`, `logger.js`, `quality-gate.js`
  - `generate-review.js`, `pipeline-auteurs.js`, `sitemap-generate.js`
  - `export-titles.js`, `import-titles.js`, etc.
- `prompts/` — All LLM prompts (greenfield-stage1.txt, greenfield-stage2.txt, pass1-*.txt, etc.)

#### `config/`
- `models.json` + environment variants (`models-dev.json`, `models-test.json`, `models-prod.json`)
- `advice.json`, `auteurs.json`

#### `data/`
**Primary data layer**
- `data/reviews/*.json` → **Canonical source of truth** for all live reviews (162+ files)
- `data/greenfield/`
  - `inputs/` → Minimal input files for new reviews
  - `stage1/` → Factual metadata from Stage 1 (Haiku)
  - `stage2/` → Full editorial review JSON from Stage 2 (Sonnet)
- `data/schemas/` → JSON schema definitions
- `data/reports/gsc/` and `analytics/` → Performance exports
- `extracted_metadata.csv`, `titles-export.csv`, etc.

#### `pipeline/` (Working directories)
- `pipeline/1-extracted/` → Legacy raw extraction (mostly historical now)
- `pipeline/2-revised/` → Final revised JSON ready for rendering (copied from greenfield or revise)
- `pipeline/3-rendered/` → Final HTML output before copying to root

#### `reviews/`
- Mirror/copy of deployed HTML files (some duplication exists due to deployment process)
- `reviews-to-review/` → Quarantine folder for problematic or legacy pages

#### `docs/`
Strategy, documentation, and reference material.
- `completed-tasks.md`, `ROADMAP-TECHNICAL.md`, `ROADMAP-PRODUCT.md`
- `business-strategy.md`, `project-objectives.md`, `pipeline-notes.md`
- `review-schema.md`, `POLICIES.md`, `project-structure.md` (this file)
- Archived advice and consolidated docs

#### `images/`
- Book cover images named exactly after slug (`the-godfather.jpg`, etc.)

#### `logs/`
- Timestamped logs from all pipeline runs

#### `archive/`
- Old scripts, legacy HTML, and superseded files

---

### Current Pipeline Flows

**Greenfield (New Review)**
1. Create minimal JSON → `data/greenfield/inputs/`
2. `pipeline-generate.js` → Stage 1 (facts) → Stage 2 (full content)
3. Output → `pipeline/2-revised/`
4. Titles pass + Pass 2 → `pipeline-render.js`
5. HTML copied to root

**Revision (Existing Review)**
`pipeline-revise.js` → `pipeline-render.js`

**Homepage**
`pipeline-browse.js` reads from `pipeline/2-revised/` → generates `index.html`

---

### Cleanup Checklist (Post-Transition)

Now that we have successfully transitioned the majority of existing movies through the full pipeline (JSON-first + greenfield), here is the recommended cleanup plan:

**High Priority (Do this week)**
1. Archive or delete old raw HTML files from root (keep only generated files like `index.html`, `auteurs.html`, `about.html`).
2. Formally declare `data/reviews/*.json` as the single canonical source of truth. Update `POLICIES.md` and `pipeline-notes.md`.
3. Move `pipeline/1-extracted/` contents to `archive/` or delete if no longer needed.

**Medium Priority**
4. Decide on `reviews/` folder: either remove duplication or document its purpose clearly.
5. Clean up empty or obsolete folders (e.g. `data/greenfield/outputs/` if unused).
6. Review and archive very old files in `docs/Other LLM Advice/` and `consolidated-docs.txt` if they are superseded.

**Low Priority / Ongoing**
7. Implement log rotation/cleanup for `logs/`.
8. Ensure every review in `data/reviews/` has a matching image in `images/` (add a validation check).

**Recommended Commit Message After Cleanup:**
"Cleanup: archive legacy HTML and extraction artifacts after full pipeline transition"

---

This document is now comprehensive and up-to-date. You can safely replace your old `project-structure.txt` with this version.

Would you like me to also create a short `docs/cleanup-plan.md` with exact terminal commands and git steps to execute the cleanup checklist?

===== reference\review-schema.md =====

# BooksVersusMovies.com — Review JSON Schema
=============================================
v1 — April 2026

This document defines the canonical JSON schema for review content.
One JSON file per review, stored in data/reviews/<slug>.json.

This is the source of truth for all pipeline operations:
  - html-to-json.js   reads HTML → writes this format
  - revise.js         reads this format → writes this format (revised)
  - render.js         reads this format → writes HTML
  - generate.js       writes this format from scratch (greenfield)

The renderer owns all HTML structure. The JSON owns all content.
No HTML tags should appear in any JSON field except where explicitly noted.


## Field reference

### Identity
  slug          string    URL slug, no extension. e.g. "atonement"
  filename      string    HTML filename. e.g. "atonement.html"
  lastUpdated   string    ISO date. e.g. "2026-04-11"
  pipelineVersion string  Schema version. Always "1" for this schema.

### SEO / head
  pageTitle     string    Full <title> tag content
  metaDesc      string    Meta description, 150 chars max
  reviewBody    string    One punchy sentence for Review JSON-LD schema
  ratingValue   string    "4" or "5" for Review JSON-LD schema

### Book metadata
  bookTitle     string    Title as it appears on the cover
  author        string    Author full name(s). e.g. "Ian McEwan"
  bookYear      string    Publication year. May be non-numeric e.g. "800 BC"
  genre         string    Genre tag text. e.g. "Literary Fiction / Romance"

### Adaptation metadata
  mediaType     string    "film" or "series"
  mediaLabel    string    Display label. e.g. "The Film" or "The Series"
  filmYear      string    Release year or "TBA"
  director      string    Director name(s). Null/omitted for series pages.
  starringLine  string    Starring line for trailer panel.
                          e.g. "Starring Keira Knightley, James McAvoy — Film: 2007"

### Verdict
  verdictText   string    "Book Wins" | "Too Close to Call" | "Movie Wins"
  verdictClass  string    CSS class: "verdict-book" | "verdict-tie" | "verdict-film"

### Affiliate / media
  affiliateLink      string   Primary Amazon affiliate URL (book cover + buy button)
  affiliateLinkAlt   string   Secondary affiliate link if present. Null if not.
  videoAffiliateLink string   Buy/rent video link. Null if not present.
  youtubeId          string   YouTube video ID for trailer thumbnail
  trailerUrl         string   Full YouTube URL
  bookCoverImage     string   Image filename only. e.g. "atonement.jpg"
                              Renderer prepends the correct path.

### Page content

  storyBrief    string    2-3 paragraph plot and context summary.
                          Plain text, paragraphs separated by \n\n.
                          No HTML tags.

  quickAnswer   object    NEW — added by revision pipeline Pass 1.
                          Null in extracted JSON, populated after revision.
    winner        string  "Book" | "Film" | "Series" | "Too Close to Call"
    readFirst     string  "Yes" | "No" | "Either order works"
    oneLineReason string  Single sentence. e.g. "The prose is irreplaceable."

  characters    array     Character comparison table rows.
    name          string  Character name. e.g. "Briony Tallis"
    actor         string  Actor name(s). e.g. "Saoirse Ronan / Romola Garai / Vanessa Redgrave"
    inBook        string  Character description in the book.
    inFilm        string  Character description in the adaptation.

  differences   array     Key difference sections. 4-5 items expected.
    title         string  Section heading. e.g. "Briony's interiority is the novel's greatest achievement"
    body          string  2-paragraph analysis. Paragraphs separated by \n\n. No HTML.

  readFirst     string    "Should You Read First?" section.
                          2 paragraphs separated by \n\n.

  verdictBox    string    Verdict box body text. 1 paragraph. No HTML.

  faq           array     FAQ items. 4-5 items expected.
    question      string  Question text.
    answer        string  Answer text. Plain text, no HTML.

  ctaBlocks     array     NEW — added by revision pipeline Pass 2.
                          Null in extracted JSON, populated after revision.
                          Defines additional CTA placements beyond the default buy button.
    location      string  "after-quick-answer" | "after-read-first" | "after-verdict"
    text          string  CTA link text. e.g. "Read the book first →"
    href          string  Affiliate URL. Copied from affiliateLink by default.

### Related content
  relatedSectionTitle  string  e.g. "More Literary Fiction Comparisons"
  related              array   Exactly 3 items.
    slug                 string  e.g. "gone-girl"
    title                string  Display title. e.g. "Gone Girl"

### Flags (set by extractor, not edited manually)
  hasSpoilerWarning   boolean  Whether spoiler warning block is present
  hasQuickAnswer      boolean  Whether quick-answer block is present
  hasCharTable        boolean  Whether character table is present
  generation          string   "v2" for all current pages


## Complete example (atonement.json, abbreviated)

{
  "slug": "atonement",
  "filename": "atonement.html",
  "lastUpdated": "2026-04-11",
  "pipelineVersion": "1",

  "pageTitle": "Atonement Book vs Movie: McEwan vs Joe Wright",
  "metaDesc": "Ian McEwan's 2001 novel versus Joe Wright's 2007 film starring Keira Knightley and James McAvoy. The Dunkirk sequence is cinema perfection, but the book's metafictional ending changes everything.",
  "reviewBody": "Joe Wright made one of the most beautiful films of the 2000s and it still cannot do what McEwan does. Read the book — the film will haunt you, but the novel haunts you longer.",
  "ratingValue": "5",

  "bookTitle": "Atonement",
  "author": "Ian McEwan",
  "bookYear": "2001",
  "genre": "Literary Fiction / Romance",

  "mediaType": "film",
  "mediaLabel": "The Film",
  "filmYear": "2007",
  "director": "Joe Wright",
  "starringLine": "Starring Keira Knightley, James McAvoy, Saoirse Ronan — Film: 2007",

  "verdictText": "Book Wins",
  "verdictClass": "verdict-book",

  "affiliateLink": "https://amzn.to/4bY9ZQs",
  "affiliateLinkAlt": null,
  "videoAffiliateLink": null,
  "youtubeId": "fiv8Gy5CJ0Y",
  "trailerUrl": "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fiv8Gy5CJ0Y",
  "bookCoverImage": "atonement.jpg",

  "storyBrief": "In the summer of 1935, thirteen-year-old Briony Tallis witnesses a series of events she doesn't fully understand and makes an accusation that destroys two lives...\n\nIan McEwan's novel, published in 2001, was shortlisted for the Booker Prize...\n\nBut the novel is a meditation on storytelling itself...",

  "quickAnswer": null,

  "characters": [
    {
      "name": "Briony Tallis",
      "actor": "Saoirse Ronan / Romola Garai / Vanessa Redgrave",
      "inBook": "A precocious thirteen-year-old writer whose overactive imagination...",
      "inFilm": "Ronan captures Briony's intensity and self-importance brilliantly..."
    }
  ],

  "differences": [
    {
      "title": "Briony's interiority is the novel's greatest achievement and the film's necessary sacrifice",
      "body": "The novel's first section is one of the great feats of literary ventriloquism...\n\nSaoirse Ronan is extraordinary in the film..."
    }
  ],

  "readFirst": "Yes — emphatically. The film is beautiful and the ending will move you...\n\nRead it first and the film becomes a meditation on what even a masterful adaptation must sacrifice...",

  "verdictBox": "Joe Wright made one of the most beautiful films of the 2000s and it still can't do what McEwan does...",

  "faq": [
    {
      "question": "Is the Atonement movie faithful to the book?",
      "answer": "The film follows the novel's plot closely through the first three sections..."
    }
  ],

  "ctaBlocks": null,

  "relatedSectionTitle": "More Literary Fiction Comparisons",
  "related": [
    { "slug": "gone-girl",        "title": "Gone Girl" },
    { "slug": "wuthering-heights","title": "Wuthering Heights" },
    { "slug": "hamnet",           "title": "Hamnet" }
  ],

  "hasSpoilerWarning": true,
  "hasQuickAnswer": false,
  "hasCharTable": true,
  "generation": "v2"
}


## Design decisions

1. No HTML in content fields
   All text fields contain plain text only. Paragraph breaks use \n\n.
   The renderer handles all markup. This makes content safe for any model
   to read or write without risk of malformed HTML.

2. quickAnswer and ctaBlocks are null until pipeline adds them
   Extracted JSON preserves the original page state exactly.
   Pass 1 sets quickAnswer. Pass 2 sets ctaBlocks.
   The renderer handles both null (omit the block) and populated states.

3. bookCoverImage is filename only
   The renderer constructs the full path. This makes the JSON portable
   and independent of the deployment directory structure.

4. differences.body uses \n\n for paragraph breaks
   Each difference section contains exactly 2 paragraphs in current pages.
   The renderer splits on \n\n and wraps each in <p> tags.

5. director is omitted (null) for series pages
   Series legitimately have no single director. The renderer omits the
   Director meta strip item when this field is null.

6. related is always exactly 3 items
   The renderer expects exactly 3. The extractor warns if fewer are found.
   The revision pipeline should fix missing related cards before rendering.

7. pipelineVersion is always "1" for this schema
   Increment if breaking schema changes are made in future.
   Allows the renderer to detect and reject incompatible files.


===== research\ai-vulnerability-advice.md =====

BooksVersusMovies.com — Advice
==============================
Model:     Perplexity Sonar Pro (perplexity/sonar-pro)
Generated: 2026-04-13T16:04:59.918Z
Duration:  14194ms

── Question ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

PRIMARY QUESTION
================
review https://booksversusmovies.com
this is my site. I would imagine the business is vulnerable to google AI returning results directly. 
what approach should I take to mitigate this?

── Response ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Your site is indeed vulnerable to Google AI Overviews, which compress the marketing funnel by providing synthesized answers directly to users[2]. However, there are strategic approaches you can take to mitigate traffic loss and remain competitive in this AI-driven search landscape.

## Key Vulnerabilities for Your Site

AI Overviews significantly reduce click-through rates: users encountering an AI summary click on traditional search results only 8% of the time, compared to 15% without a summary[4]. Additionally, users rarely click links within the summaries themselves (just 1% of visits)[4]. This means your site could lose substantial traffic even if your content is cited.

## Mitigation Strategies

**Build Topical Authority and Depth**

Move beyond surface-level comparisons. AI systems prioritize content that demonstrates consistent expertise and niche authority[1]. For books versus movies content, this means creating in-depth analyses that go beyond generic comparisons—original insights, detailed breakdowns of specific genres, thematic explorations, or first-hand experiences will be more likely to be cited and valued by AI systems[1].

**Optimize for AI Citation Over Traditional Rankings**

Traditional keyword rankings matter less with AI search; search intent and content quality matter more[1]. Rather than targeting top-three positions for broad keywords, focus on highly specific use cases and audiences[2]. For example, instead of "best books vs movies," create targeted content like "Why Literary Adaptations Fail: A Deep Dive Into Specific Franchises" or "How Book-to-Film Adaptations Change Character Development."

**Leverage Citations and Digital Footprint**

AI Overviews can cite content from third-party platforms as well as your own website[3]. Build mentions and citations across relevant platforms (book review sites, movie databases, literary communities, Reddit discussions) to increase your visibility in AI-generated responses[3].

**Create Content for AI's Citation Patterns**

Research shows AI systems cite lower-ranking pages (positions 21+) nearly 90% of the time[2], and they focus on the relevance and quality of specific content "chunks" rather than overall page experience[2]. This means you don't need perfect page speed or extensive content depth on every page—instead, ensure each section of content is highly relevant, well-structured, and answers specific questions clearly[1].

**Develop Conversion-Focused Strategies**

Since AI search visitors are worth 4.4x more than traditional organic visitors based on conversion rate[2], focus on converting the traffic you do receive. Even with reduced clicks, higher-value conversions (newsletter signups, community engagement, premium content access) can offset volume losses.

**Differentiate with Original Content**

AI systems show preference for business/service websites when generating responses about those businesses[2]. If you have unique perspectives, original research, or exclusive content about books and movies, emphasize this. Generic listicles will be compressed into AI summaries; original analysis will drive citations and traffic.

===== research\competitors.txt =====

# BooksVersusMovies.com — Eye of the Tiger: Competitors & Positioning
====================================================================
Last updated: April 2026

This is our "motivation + strategy" document. We don't just want to participate in the book-to-screen comparison space — we want to own it.

Our core advantage:
- Honest, spoiler-heavy, no-sugarcoating verdicts
- Clear "Book Wins / Film Wins / Too Close to Call" system
- Structured "Read First" guidance with affiliate intent

### The Competitive Landscape (April 2026)

#### Tier 1 — Direct Competitors (The ones we can realistically beat or surpass)
- **BookBub Adaptation Lists**  
  Strength: Very timely roundups, large email audience  
  Weakness: Shallow, no real verdicts, no "which should I experience first"  
  Our edge: Depth + opinion + actionable advice

- **Variety / Hollywood Reporter / Deadline**  
  Strength: Industry scoops, casting news, release dates  
  Weakness: Almost zero reader-focused comparison or honest opinion  
  Our edge: We tell readers what actually matters — the verdict

- **Rotten Tomatoes "Most Anticipated Book Adaptations"**  
  Strength: Hype + critic aggregation  
  Weakness: No structured side-by-side breakdowns  
  Our edge: Personal, consistent verdicts people can trust

- **LitHub occasional adaptation pieces**  
  Strength: Literary credibility  
  Weakness: Sporadic, not structured, not scalable  
  Our edge: Consistent format + volume

#### Tier 2 — Broader Entertainment Sites
- Collider, ScreenRant, IGN  
  They treat adaptations as news, rarely do deep comparisons.

- Goodreads user lists ("Best Book to Movie Adaptations")  
  High traffic but chaotic and low quality.

- Consistent opinionated verdicts
